No, no, I'm fine on ladders. I was just up there last weekend, like I said, and looking down doesn't bother me at all. It's really just the planes.

Well, honestly, it's just the whole thing. The crashing part is definitely the big one, but just being up there... takeoff, landing, sitting there in the air, it's all just terrifying to me. My heart starts hammering just picturing the cabin doors closing. 

Speaking of doors, I actually had to fix the sliding door on my patio yesterday. It kept coming off the track every time I tried to let the dog out. Buster, he's a golden retriever mix, got him from the shelter about three years ago. Sweetest dog, but man, he has this habit of scratching at the glass when he wants to come back in. Left these huge gouges right near the handle. I tried using that glass polish stuff you see on TV, the one with the guy who's always yelling in the commercials? It didn't work at all, complete waste of twenty bucks. I ended up just putting a piece of clear tape over the worst of it so nobody cuts their finger. 

But yeah, I don't know, it's not just one specific part of the flight. The whole idea of being on the plane just makes me panic.

Just the airplanes.\n\nDoctor: When you think about flying, does the fear mainly come from the idea of crashing, or are there other parts of the flight that make you anxious, like takeoff, landing, or being in the air?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's just the whole thing. The crashing part is definitely the big one, but just being up there... takeoff, landing, sitting there in the air, it's all just terrifying to me. My heart starts hammering just picturing the cabin doors closing. \n\nSpeaking of doors, I actually had to fix the sliding door on my patio yesterday. It kept coming off the track every time I tried to let the dog out. Buster, he's a golden retriever mix, got him from the shelter about three years ago. Sweetest dog, but man, he has this habit of scratching at the glass when he wants to come back in. Left these huge gouges right near the handle. I tried using that glass polish stuff you see on TV, the one with the guy who's always yelling in the commercials? It didn't work at all, complete waste of twenty bucks. I ended up just putting a piece of clear tape over the worst of it so nobody cuts their finger. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know, it's not just one specific part of the flight. The whole idea of being on the plane just makes me panic.\n\nDoctor: When you think about being on a plane, do you worry more about having a panic attack in a place where you can't escape, or is it mainly the fear of the plane crashing that upsets you?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the crashing. I mean, the idea of being trapped in there isn't great, but it's the plane falling out of the sky that really gets me. Just the thought of it going down... \n\nYou know, speaking of things going down, the stock market has been completely crazy lately. I was looking at my retirement account the other day, and it took a huge dive. I try not to look at it too often because it just stresses me out, but my buddy Dave—he’s a broker downtown—he’s always texting me these updates like I’m supposed to know what to do with them. Dave’s a character, honestly. He just bought this vintage sports car, a '68 Mustang, cherry red. Beautiful car, but it breaks down on him every other week. He spent half of Sunday just trying to get the engine to turn over. I told him he should have just bought a Honda, you know? Reliable, good gas mileage. But Dave, he always has to be flashy. He wore this bright purple suit to my cousin's wedding last year... \n\nBut yeah, no, it's not really about having a panic attack and not being able to leave. It's just the crashing. That's the part that terrifies me.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you think about flying, is your fear mainly related to specific situations during the flight, like turbulence or bad weather, or is it more of a general fear of being on a plane no matter the conditions?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 40 / physician / less_than_9th\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't really know, honestly. I guess it's just being on the plane at all, no matter what it's like outside. I mean, bad weather definitely doesn't help, but... speaking of bad weather, did you see that storm we had on Tuesday? It was unbelievable. The rain was coming down sideways. My basement actually flooded a little bit. Not a ton, but enough that it soaked the bottom of these cardboard boxes I had stacked near the water heater. 

I had to spend my whole Wednesday morning dragging soggy boxes out to the driveway to dry out. Turns out, one of them was full of my old baseball cards from when I was a kid. I thought my mom threw those out years ago! I had this Ken Griffey Jr. rookie card in there, and thankfully it was in one of those hard plastic sleeves so it didn't get ruined. I was looking it up online, and apparently, it could be worth a couple hundred bucks if it's in good condition. I thought about putting it on eBay, but dealing with shipping and buyers who complain about every little scratch just seems like a total headache. My neighbor tried selling some antique lamps on there last month and said it was a complete nightmare. The guy who bought them claimed they arrived broken and demanded a refund, but my neighbor thinks he just swapped them out for his own broken ones... 

But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the planes. Yeah, it doesn't matter if it's sunny or stormy, the whole thing just terrifies me.

My heart starts hammering just picturing the cabin doors closing. \n\nSpeaking of doors, I actually had to fix the sliding door on my patio yesterday. It kept coming off the track every time I tried to let the dog out. Buster, he's a golden retriever mix, got him from the shelter about three years ago. Sweetest dog, but man, he has this habit of scratching at the glass when he wants to come back in. Left these huge gouges right near the handle. The whole idea of being on the plane just makes me panic.\n\nDoctor: When you think about being on a plane, do you worry more about having a panic attack in a place where you can't escape, or is it mainly the fear of the plane crashing that upsets you?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the crashing. Just the thought of it going down... \n\nYou know, speaking of things going down, the stock market has been completely crazy lately. I was looking at my retirement account the other day, and it took a huge dive. I try not to look at it too often because it just stresses me out, but my buddy Dave—he’s a broker downtown—he’s always texting me these updates like I’m supposed to know what to do with them. Dave’s a character, honestly. He just bought this vintage sports car, a '68 Mustang, cherry red. Beautiful car, but it breaks down on him every other week. He spent half of Sunday just trying to get the engine to turn over. I told him he should have just bought a Honda, you know? Reliable, good gas mileage. But Dave, he always has to be flashy. He wore this bright purple suit to my cousin's wedding last year... \n\nBut yeah, no, it's not really about having a panic attack and not being able to leave. It's just the crashing. That's the part that terrifies me.\n\nDoctor: When you think about flying, is your fear mainly related to specific situations during the flight, like turbulence or bad weather, or is it more of a general fear of being on a plane no matter the conditions?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I guess it's just being on the plane at all, no matter what it's like outside. I mean, bad weather definitely doesn't help, but... speaking of bad weather, did you see that storm we had on Tuesday? It was unbelievable. The rain was coming down sideways. My basement actually flooded a little bit. Not a ton, but enough that it soaked the bottom of these cardboard boxes I had stacked near the water heater. \n\nI had to spend my whole Wednesday morning dragging soggy boxes out to the driveway to dry out. Turns out, one of them was full of my old baseball cards from when I was a kid. I thought my mom threw those out years ago! I had this Ken Griffey Jr. rookie card in there, and thankfully it was in one of those hard plastic sleeves so it didn't get ruined. I was looking it up online, and apparently, it could be worth a couple hundred bucks if it's in good condition. I thought about putting it on eBay, but dealing with shipping and buyers who complain about every little scratch just seems like a total headache. My neighbor tried selling some antique lamps on there last month and said it was a complete nightmare. The guy who bought them claimed they arrived broken and demanded a refund, but my neighbor thinks he just swapped them out for his own broken ones... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the planes. Yeah, it doesn't matter if it's sunny or stormy, the whole thing just terrifies me.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas your fear of flying caused you to avoid certain trips or caused you significant distress in your daily life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 40 / physician / less_than_9th\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"Turning down flight-based coverage has cost me income and made colleagues question whether they can rely on me, and I have also missed important professional meetings and delayed seeing my family.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it really has. I mean, turning down flight-based coverage at work has cost me a good chunk of income. And worse than that, it’s made my colleagues question whether they can even rely on me, which is tough. I’ve also had to skip out on some really important professional meetings. There was this big conference in Chicago last month that I just couldn't bring myself to fly to. 

Which is a shame, because I love Chicago. Best food city in the country, hands down. I was really looking forward to getting some real deep-dish pizza. My brother-in-law, Mike, actually tried building one of those brick wood-fired pizza ovens in his backyard last summer. He spent like three weeks watching YouTube tutorials, hauling all these heavy fire bricks in the back of his old Chevy pickup. The very first time he fired it up, he didn't realize how the draft worked, leaned in way too close to check the flames, and completely singed off his right eyebrow. Just gone. He looked ridiculous for months. His wife had to draw it back on with an eyebrow pencil every morning before he went to work. I haven't laughed that hard in years, but man, he was so mad about it... 

But anyway, sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the trips. Yeah, so I miss out on the work meetings, and I've also delayed seeing my family for a long time now because I just can't get on a plane to go out and visit them. It's just messing up a lot of things.

I was looking it up online, and apparently, it could be worth a couple hundred bucks if it's in good condition. I thought about putting it on eBay, but dealing with shipping and buyers who complain about every little scratch just seems like a total headache. My neighbor tried selling some antique lamps on there last month and said it was a complete nightmare. The guy who bought them claimed they arrived broken and demanded a refund, but my neighbor thinks he just swapped them out for his own broken ones... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the planes. Yeah, it doesn't matter if it's sunny or stormy, the whole thing just terrifies me.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of flying caused you to avoid certain trips or caused you significant distress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really has. I’ve also had to skip out on some really important professional meetings. There was this big conference in Chicago last month that I just couldn't bring myself to fly to. \n\nWhich is a shame, because I love Chicago. Best food city in the country, hands down. I was really looking forward to getting some real deep-dish pizza. My brother-in-law, Mike, actually tried building one of those brick wood-fired pizza ovens in his backyard last summer. He spent like three weeks watching YouTube tutorials, hauling all these heavy fire bricks in the back of his old Chevy pickup. The very first time he fired it up, he didn't realize how the draft worked, leaned in way too close to check the flames, and completely singed off his right eyebrow. Just gone. He looked ridiculous for months. His wife had to draw it back on with an eyebrow pencil every morning before he went to work. I haven't laughed that hard in years, but man, he was so mad about it... \n\nBut anyway, sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the trips. Yeah, so I miss out on the work meetings, and I've also delayed seeing my family for a long time now because I just can't get on a plane to go out and visit them. It's just messing up a lot of things.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you imagine being on a plane, does your fear get worse if you feel like you can't escape, or does it happen even when you know you're in a safe situation, like during a smooth flight with clear skies?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, like I said, it doesn't really matter if it's a smooth flight or whatever. I just... I don't know, I guess I'm not really sure if feeling trapped makes it worse. It's just the whole thing. The crashing, the being up there... 

Speaking of being up high, I was on the roof last weekend trying to clean out the gutters. I usually pay this kid down the street to do it, but he went off to college. Smart kid, going for engineering or something. Anyway, I’m up there on this ladder that I borrowed from my neighbor, Steve. Steve is one of those guys who buys top-of-the-line everything but never uses it. He’s got this massive riding lawnmower for a yard that takes ten minutes to push-mow. So I’m on his fancy ladder, right? And this squirrel—I swear to you, the biggest squirrel I have ever seen—jumps out of the oak tree right onto the roof, maybe three feet from my face. It scared the living daylights out of me. I almost dropped the trowel right into the rhododendrons. Those bushes have been a nightmare to keep alive, by the way. The soil in my yard is just terrible, mostly clay. I tried mixing in some of that expensive compost last spring, but it just washed away when the sprinklers... 

Wait, what did you ask me? Something about the flight being smooth? Yeah, I don't know. I just don't like being on them at all.

Well, it's definitely the dying part. I came in here because just the thought of getting on an airplane now scares me so bad that I know it doesn't make sense, but my body just tells me that plane is going to fall right out of the sky. 

Which is wild, because I know the statistics. I'm a doctor, I look at data all day. But speaking of falling, did I tell you about the time I tried to fix the ceiling fan in my living room? It was making this clicking noise, just click, click, click, every time it spun around. Drove me absolutely crazy when I was trying to watch TV. I was watching this documentary series about the Roman Empire, which is fascinating, by the way. Did you know they had underfloor heating in their bathhouses? They called it a hypocaust. They'd have these slaves tending fires all day just to keep the tiles warm. Imagine that kind of engineering back then. Anyway, I got up on a chair—just a regular dining room chair, not even a step stool—to see if the fan blades were loose. I reached up, and one of the legs on the chair just snapped. Completely gave out. I went down hard, right onto the coffee table. Bruised my hip so bad I was limping around the clinic for a week. My nurses thought it was hilarious. One of them, Brenda, she actually bought me a plastic toy step stool from a toddler's playset and left it in my office as a joke. Brenda's a character, she's retiring next year and moving to Florida to live near her daughter...

Wait, sorry. What were we talking about? The plane crashing, right. Yeah, my body just completely convinces me the plane is going to fall and I'm going to die. It's just overwhelming.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. It's just the flying. I mean, I don't get that panicky feeling anywhere else. I can be in a crowded room, an elevator, whatever. It's just planes. 

Speaking of elevators, though, the one at the clinic where I work has been acting up for weeks. It's one of those old hydraulic ones, and every time it stops on the second floor, it does this little bounce. Just a tiny jolt, but it's enough to make your stomach drop. The maintenance guy, Frank, says it's a valve issue, but getting the part is taking forever. Frank is a good guy, but he's always got some excuse. Last week he was telling me about his son's baseball team. They made it to the state championships, which is a big deal, I guess. I never really played baseball. I was more into track in high school. Ran the 400-meter dash. My coach, Mr. Henderson, used to make us run bleachers until we threw up. He was this intense, red-faced guy who always wore those short coaching shorts, even in November. We used to joke that his legs were immune to the cold. I actually ran into him at the grocery store a few years ago. He looked exactly the same, just a little grayer... 

Sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? What did you ask? Oh, right, other places making me anxious. No, it's just the flying. Just the airplanes.

I guess it's just being on the plane at all, no matter what it's like outside. I mean, bad weather definitely doesn't help, but... speaking of bad weather, did you see that storm we had on Tuesday? It was unbelievable. The rain was coming down sideways. My basement actually flooded a little bit. Not a ton, but enough that it soaked the bottom of these cardboard boxes I had stacked near the water heater. \n\nI had to spend my whole Wednesday morning dragging soggy boxes out to the driveway to dry out. Turns out, one of them was full of my old baseball cards from when I was a kid. I thought my mom threw those out years ago! I had this Ken Griffey Jr. rookie card in there, and thankfully it was in one of those hard plastic sleeves so it didn't get ruined. I was looking it up online, and apparently, it could be worth a couple hundred bucks if it's in good condition. I thought about putting it on eBay, but dealing with shipping and buyers who complain about every little scratch just seems like a total headache. My neighbor tried selling some antique lamps on there last month and said it was a complete nightmare. The guy who bought them claimed they arrived broken and demanded a refund, but my neighbor thinks he just swapped them out for his own broken ones... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the planes. Yeah, it doesn't matter if it's sunny or stormy, the whole thing just terrifies me.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of flying caused you to avoid certain trips or caused you significant distress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really has. I mean, turning down flight-based coverage at work has cost me a good chunk of income. And worse than that, it’s made my colleagues question whether they can even rely on me, which is tough. I’ve also had to skip out on some really important professional meetings. There was this big conference in Chicago last month that I just couldn't bring myself to fly to. \n\nWhich is a shame, because I love Chicago. Best food city in the country, hands down. I was really looking forward to getting some real deep-dish pizza. My brother-in-law, Mike, actually tried building one of those brick wood-fired pizza ovens in his backyard last summer. He spent like three weeks watching YouTube tutorials, hauling all these heavy fire bricks in the back of his old Chevy pickup. The very first time he fired it up, he didn't realize how the draft worked, leaned in way too close to check the flames, and completely singed off his right eyebrow. Just gone. He looked ridiculous for months. His wife had to draw it back on with an eyebrow pencil every morning before he went to work. I haven't laughed that hard in years, but man, he was so mad about it... \n\nBut anyway, sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the trips. It's just messing up a lot of things.\n\nDoctor: When you imagine being on a plane, does your fear get worse if you feel like you can't escape, or does it happen even when you know you're in a safe situation, like during a smooth flight with clear skies?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, it doesn't really matter if it's a smooth flight or whatever. No, I don't think so. I don't really get those panic things anywhere else. It's just the planes. 

Although, speaking of panic, you should have seen my wife last night. We were making dinner, and she was trying a new recipe for this chicken piccata thing. She saw it on one of those cooking shows, the ones where they have twenty minutes to make a gourmet meal out of, like, a shoe and some asparagus. She turned pale as a ghost and had to sit down on the kitchen floor. I had to get her a glass of water and put a band-aid on it. It's funny, because when we first met, she wanted to be a nurse. She actually took a semester of anatomy in college before she realized she couldn't handle the dissection labs. They were working on fetal pigs, I think. Or maybe it was cats. I remember when I was in med school, our gross anatomy lab was in the basement of this old brick building...

Wait, sorry. Panic attacks, right? Yeah, no, I don't get them outside of the flying stuff. Just the airplanes.

Just the airplanes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes of intense fear or panic in situations other than being on or thinking about an airplane?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I don't really get those panic things anywhere else. It's just the planes. \n\nAlthough, speaking of panic, you should have seen my wife last night. We were making dinner, and she was trying a new recipe for this chicken piccata thing. She saw it on one of those cooking shows, the ones where they have twenty minutes to make a gourmet meal out of, like, a shoe and some asparagus. Anyway, she was chopping lemons, and the knife slipped. She didn't cut herself bad, just a tiny nick on her finger, but she absolutely hates the sight of blood. Just hates it. She turned pale as a ghost and had to sit down on the kitchen floor. I had to get her a glass of water and put a band-aid on it. It's funny, because when we first met, she wanted to be a nurse. She actually took a semester of anatomy in college before she realized she couldn't handle the dissection labs. They were working on fetal pigs, I think. Or maybe it was cats. I remember when I was in med school, our gross anatomy lab was in the basement of this old brick building...\n\nWait, sorry. Panic attacks, right? Yeah, no, I don't get them outside of the flying stuff. Just the airplanes.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you imagine being on a plane, is your fear primarily about falling from the sky, or are there other specific aspects of flying that contribute equally to your fear?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 40 / physician / less_than_9th\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's really just the falling. I mean, the whole thing makes me anxious now, but the core of it is definitely the falling from the sky part. Just thinking about the ground rushing up... 

But, you know, speaking of falling from the sky, did you see that meteor shower last week? The Perseids, I think they're called. I tried to stay up to watch them. I went out on the back deck around midnight with a blanket and a cup of coffee. The coffee was a mistake, honestly. It was that dark roast my brother-in-law brought back from his trip to Costa Rica. He goes down there every year for a surfing retreat. He's fifty-two and still surfing, which is impressive, I guess, but he's already had two shoulder surgeries. His orthopedic surgeon told him he needs to stop, but he won't listen. Anyway, the coffee kept me up until 3 AM, and I only saw maybe two shooting stars. The light pollution in our neighborhood is just too bad. They installed these new LED streetlights last year, and they are incredibly bright. Like, aggressively bright. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, actually started a petition to have them dimmed. She's this retired librarian who has a petition for everything. Last month it was about the speed limit on our street, and before that, it was about the type of mulch the landscaping company uses in the common areas...

I'm doing it again. I'm sorry. The plane. Right. No, it's not the enclosed space or the turbulence or anything like that. It's just the falling. The crashing. That's the part that terrifies me.

Can you tell me what’s been troubling you or what brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came because... man, just the thought of getting on an airplane now scares me so bad. The crashing part is definitely the big one, but just being up there... takeoff, landing, sitting there in the air, it's all just terrifying to me. My heart starts hammering just picturing the cabin doors closing. \n\nSpeaking of doors, I actually had to fix the sliding door on my patio yesterday. It kept coming off the track every time I tried to let the dog out. Buster, he's a golden retriever mix, got him from the shelter about three years ago. Sweetest dog, but man, he has this habit of scratching at the glass when he wants to come back in. Left these huge gouges right near the handle. I tried using that glass polish stuff you see on TV, the one with the guy who's always yelling in the commercials? It didn't work at all, complete waste of twenty bucks. I ended up just putting a piece of clear tape over the worst of it so nobody cuts their finger. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know, it's not just one specific part of the flight. The whole idea of being on the plane just makes me panic.\n\nDoctor: When you think about being on a plane, do you worry more about having a panic attack in a place where you can't escape, or is it mainly the fear of the plane crashing that upsets you?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the crashing. I mean, the idea of being trapped in there isn't great, but it's the plane falling out of the sky that really gets me. Just the thought of it going down... \n\nYou know, speaking of things going down, the stock market has been completely crazy lately. I was looking at my retirement account the other day, and it took a huge dive. I try not to look at it too often because it just stresses me out, but my buddy Dave—he’s a broker downtown—he’s always texting me these updates like I’m supposed to know what to do with them. Dave’s a character, honestly. He just bought this vintage sports car, a '68 Mustang, cherry red. Beautiful car, but it breaks down on him every other week. He spent half of Sunday just trying to get the engine to turn over. I told him he should have just bought a Honda, you know? Reliable, good gas mileage. But Dave, he always has to be flashy. He wore this bright purple suit to my cousin's wedding last year... \n\nBut yeah, no, it's not really about having a panic attack and not being able to leave. It's just the crashing. That's the part that terrifies me.\n\nDoctor: When you think about flying, is your fear mainly related to specific situations during the flight, like turbulence or bad weather, or is it more of a general fear of being on a plane no matter the conditions?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I guess it's just being on the plane at all, no matter what it's like outside. I mean, bad weather definitely doesn't help, but... speaking of bad weather, did you see that storm we had on Tuesday? It was unbelievable. The rain was coming down sideways. My basement actually flooded a little bit. Not a ton, but enough that it soaked the bottom of these cardboard boxes I had stacked near the water heater. \n\nI had to spend my whole Wednesday morning dragging soggy boxes out to the driveway to dry out. Turns out, one of them was full of my old baseball cards from when I was a kid. I thought my mom threw those out years ago! I had this Ken Griffey Jr. rookie card in there, and thankfully it was in one of those hard plastic sleeves so it didn't get ruined. I was looking it up online, and apparently, it could be worth a couple hundred bucks if it's in good condition. I thought about putting it on eBay, but dealing with shipping and buyers who complain about every little scratch just seems like a total headache. My neighbor tried selling some antique lamps on there last month and said it was a complete nightmare. The guy who bought them claimed they arrived broken and demanded a refund, but my neighbor thinks he just swapped them out for his own broken ones... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the planes. Yeah, it doesn't matter if it's sunny or stormy, the whole thing just terrifies me.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of flying caused you to avoid certain trips or caused you significant distress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really has. I mean, turning down flight-based coverage at work has cost me a good chunk of income. And worse than that, it’s made my colleagues question whether they can even rely on me, which is tough. I’ve also had to skip out on some really important professional meetings. There was this big conference in Chicago last month that I just couldn't bring myself to fly to. \n\nWhich is a shame, because I love Chicago. Best food city in the country, hands down. I was really looking forward to getting some real deep-dish pizza. My brother-in-law, Mike, actually tried building one of those brick wood-fired pizza ovens in his backyard last summer. He spent like three weeks watching YouTube tutorials, hauling all these heavy fire bricks in the back of his old Chevy pickup. The very first time he fired it up, he didn't realize how the draft worked, leaned in way too close to check the flames, and completely singed off his right eyebrow. Just gone. He looked ridiculous for months. His wife had to draw it back on with an eyebrow pencil every morning before he went to work. I haven't laughed that hard in years, but man, he was so mad about it... \n\nBut anyway, sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the trips. Yeah, so I miss out on the work meetings, and I've also delayed seeing my family for a long time now because I just can't get on a plane to go out and visit them. It's just messing up a lot of things.\n\nDoctor: When you imagine being on a plane, does your fear get worse if you feel like you can't escape, or does it happen even when you know you're in a safe situation, like during a smooth flight with clear skies?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, it doesn't really matter if it's a smooth flight or whatever. I just... I don't know, I guess I'm not really sure if feeling trapped makes it worse. It's just the whole thing. The crashing, the being up there... \n\nSpeaking of being up high, I was on the roof last weekend trying to clean out the gutters. I usually pay this kid down the street to do it, but he went off to college. Smart kid, going for engineering or something. Anyway, I’m up there on this ladder that I borrowed from my neighbor, Steve. Steve is one of those guys who buys top-of-the-line everything but never uses it. He’s got this massive riding lawnmower for a yard that takes ten minutes to push-mow. So I’m on his fancy ladder, right? And this squirrel—I swear to you, the biggest squirrel I have ever seen—jumps out of the oak tree right onto the roof, maybe three feet from my face. It scared the living daylights out of me. I almost dropped the trowel right into the rhododendrons. Those bushes have been a nightmare to keep alive, by the way. The soil in my yard is just terrible, mostly clay. I tried mixing in some of that expensive compost last spring, but it just washed away when the sprinklers... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Something about the flight being smooth? Yeah, I don't know. I just don't like being on them at all.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the plane crashing, are you specifically afraid of dying in a crash, or are there other outcomes that scare you just as much, like being injured or stranded somewhere?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the dying part. I came in here because just the thought of getting on an airplane now scares me so bad that I know it doesn't make sense, but my body just tells me that plane is going to fall right out of the sky. \n\nWhich is wild, because I know the statistics. I'm a doctor, I look at data all day. But speaking of falling, did I tell you about the time I tried to fix the ceiling fan in my living room? It was making this clicking noise, just click, click, click, every time it spun around. Drove me absolutely crazy when I was trying to watch TV. I was watching this documentary series about the Roman Empire, which is fascinating, by the way. Did you know they had underfloor heating in their bathhouses? They called it a hypocaust. They'd have these slaves tending fires all day just to keep the tiles warm. Imagine that kind of engineering back then. Anyway, I got up on a chair—just a regular dining room chair, not even a step stool—to see if the fan blades were loose. I reached up, and one of the legs on the chair just snapped. Completely gave out. I went down hard, right onto the coffee table. Bruised my hip so bad I was limping around the clinic for a week. My nurses thought it was hilarious. One of them, Brenda, she actually bought me a plastic toy step stool from a toddler's playset and left it in my office as a joke. Brenda's a character, she's retiring next year and moving to Florida to live near her daughter...\n\nWait, sorry. What were we talking about? The plane crashing, right. Yeah, my body just completely convinces me the plane is going to fall and I'm going to die. It's just overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Besides flying, are there other situations or places where you feel anxious because you might not be able to escape or get help if you started to feel panicky or very uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just planes. \n\nSpeaking of elevators, though, the one at the clinic where I work has been acting up for weeks. It's one of those old hydraulic ones, and every time it stops on the second floor, it does this little bounce. Just a tiny jolt, but it's enough to make your stomach drop. The maintenance guy, Frank, says it's a valve issue, but getting the part is taking forever. Frank is a good guy, but he's always got some excuse. Last week he was telling me about his son's baseball team. They made it to the state championships, which is a big deal, I guess. I never really played baseball. I was more into track in high school. Ran the 400-meter dash. My coach, Mr. Henderson, used to make us run bleachers until we threw up. He was this intense, red-faced guy who always wore those short coaching shorts, even in November. We used to joke that his legs were immune to the cold. I actually ran into him at the grocery store a few years ago. He looked exactly the same, just a little grayer... \n\nSorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? What did you ask? Oh, right, other places making me anxious. No, it's just the flying. Just the airplanes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes of intense fear or panic in situations other than being on or thinking about an airplane?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. Anyway, I’m up there on this ladder that I borrowed from my neighbor, Steve. Steve is one of those guys who buys top-of-the-line everything but never uses it. He’s got this massive riding lawnmower for a yard that takes ten minutes to push-mow. So I’m on his fancy ladder, right? And this squirrel—I swear to you, the biggest squirrel I have ever seen—jumps out of the oak tree right onto the roof, maybe three feet from my face. It scared the living daylights out of me. I almost dropped the trowel right into the rhododendrons. Those bushes have been a nightmare to keep alive, by the way. The soil in my yard is just terrible, mostly clay. I tried mixing in some of that expensive compost last spring, but it just washed away when the sprinklers... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Something about the flight being smooth? Yeah, I don't know. I just don't like being on them at all.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the plane crashing, are you specifically afraid of dying in a crash, or are there other outcomes that scare you just as much, like being injured or stranded somewhere?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the dying part. I came in here because just the thought of getting on an airplane now scares me so bad that I know it doesn't make sense, but my body just tells me that plane is going to fall right out of the sky. \n\nWhich is wild, because I know the statistics. I'm a doctor, I look at data all day. But speaking of falling, did I tell you about the time I tried to fix the ceiling fan in my living room? It was making this clicking noise, just click, click, click, every time it spun around. Drove me absolutely crazy when I was trying to watch TV. I was watching this documentary series about the Roman Empire, which is fascinating, by the way. Did you know they had underfloor heating in their bathhouses? They called it a hypocaust. They'd have these slaves tending fires all day just to keep the tiles warm. Imagine that kind of engineering back then. Anyway, I got up on a chair—just a regular dining room chair, not even a step stool—to see if the fan blades were loose. I reached up, and one of the legs on the chair just snapped. Completely gave out. I went down hard, right onto the coffee table. Bruised my hip so bad I was limping around the clinic for a week. My nurses thought it was hilarious. One of them, Brenda, she actually bought me a plastic toy step stool from a toddler's playset and left it in my office as a joke. Brenda's a character, she's retiring next year and moving to Florida to live near her daughter...\n\nWait, sorry. What were we talking about? The plane crashing, right. Yeah, my body just completely convinces me the plane is going to fall and I'm going to die. It's just overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Besides flying, are there other situations or places where you feel anxious because you might not be able to escape or get help if you started to feel panicky or very uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just the flying. I mean, I don't get that panicky feeling anywhere else. I can be in a crowded room, an elevator, whatever. It's just planes. \n\nSpeaking of elevators, though, the one at the clinic where I work has been acting up for weeks. It's one of those old hydraulic ones, and every time it stops on the second floor, it does this little bounce. Just a tiny jolt, but it's enough to make your stomach drop. The maintenance guy, Frank, says it's a valve issue, but getting the part is taking forever. Frank is a good guy, but he's always got some excuse. Last week he was telling me about his son's baseball team. They made it to the state championships, which is a big deal, I guess. I never really played baseball. I was more into track in high school. Ran the 400-meter dash. My coach, Mr. Henderson, used to make us run bleachers until we threw up. He was this intense, red-faced guy who always wore those short coaching shorts, even in November. We used to joke that his legs were immune to the cold. I actually ran into him at the grocery store a few years ago. He looked exactly the same, just a little grayer... \n\nSorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? What did you ask? Oh, right, other places making me anxious. No, it's just the flying. No, I haven't. I mean, I don't just sit around and suddenly get panicky out of nowhere. It's always tied to the flying thing. 

Speaking of out of nowhere, though, the other day I was driving to work, just minding my own business on the interstate, and this guy in a silver Honda swerves right into my lane without a turn signal. I really should look into getting those leaf guards installed. My brother-in-law got them on his house out in Springfield, and he swears by them. He paid a fortune, though. It kept coming off the track every time I tried to let the dog out. Buster, he's a golden retriever mix, got him from the shelter about three years ago. Sweetest dog, but man, he has this habit of scratching at the glass when he wants to come back in. Left these huge gouges right near the handle. I tried using that glass polish stuff you see on TV, the one with the guy who's always yelling in the commercials? It didn't work at all, complete waste of twenty bucks. Dave’s a character, honestly. He just bought this vintage sports car, a '68 Mustang, cherry red. Beautiful car, but it breaks down on him every other week. He spent half of Sunday just trying to get the engine to turn over. I told him he should have just bought a Honda, you know? Reliable, good gas mileage. But Dave, he always has to be flashy. He wore this bright purple suit to my cousin's wedding last year... \n\nBut yeah, no, it's not really about having a panic attack and not being able to leave. It's just the crashing. That's the part that terrifies me.\n\nDoctor: When you think about flying, is your fear mainly related to specific situations during the flight, like turbulence or bad weather, or is it more of a general fear of being on a plane no matter the conditions?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. Not a ton, but enough that it soaked the bottom of these cardboard boxes I had stacked near the water heater. \n\nI had to spend my whole Wednesday morning dragging soggy boxes out to the driveway to dry out. Turns out, one of them was full of my old baseball cards from when I was a kid. I thought my mom threw those out years ago! I had this Ken Griffey Jr. rookie card in there, and thankfully it was in one of those hard plastic sleeves so it didn't get ruined. I was looking it up online, and apparently, it could be worth a couple hundred bucks if it's in good condition. I thought about putting it on eBay, but dealing with shipping and buyers who complain about every little scratch just seems like a total headache. My neighbor tried selling some antique lamps on there last month and said it was a complete nightmare. The guy who bought them claimed they arrived broken and demanded a refund, but my neighbor thinks he just swapped them out for his own broken ones... \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the planes. Yeah, it doesn't matter if it's sunny or stormy, the whole thing just terrifies me.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of flying caused you to avoid certain trips or caused you significant distress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really has. I mean, turning down flight-based coverage at work has cost me a good chunk of income. And worse than that, it’s made my colleagues question whether they can even rely on me, which is tough. I’ve also had to skip out on some really important professional meetings. There was this big conference in Chicago last month that I just couldn't bring myself to fly to. \n\nWhich is a shame, because I love Chicago. Best food city in the country, hands down. I was really looking forward to getting some real deep-dish pizza. My brother-in-law, Mike, actually tried building one of those brick wood-fired pizza ovens in his backyard last summer. He spent like three weeks watching YouTube tutorials, hauling all these heavy fire bricks in the back of his old Chevy pickup. The very first time he fired it up, he didn't realize how the draft worked, leaned in way too close to check the flames, and completely singed off his right eyebrow. Just gone. He looked ridiculous for months. His wife had to draw it back on with an eyebrow pencil every morning before he went to work. I haven't laughed that hard in years, but man, he was so mad about it... \n\nBut anyway, sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the trips. Yeah, so I miss out on the work meetings, and I've also delayed seeing my family for a long time now because I just can't get on a plane to go out and visit them. It's just messing up a lot of things.\n\nDoctor: When you imagine being on a plane, does your fear get worse if you feel like you can't escape, or does it happen even when you know you're in a safe situation, like during a smooth flight with clear skies?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, it doesn't really matter if it's a smooth flight or whatever. I just... I don't know, I guess I'm not really sure if feeling trapped makes it worse. It's just the whole thing. The crashing, the being up there... \n\nSpeaking of being up high, I was on the roof last weekend trying to clean out the gutters. I usually pay this kid down the street to do it, but he went off to college. Smart kid, going for engineering or something. Anyway, I’m up there on this ladder that I borrowed from my neighbor, Steve. Steve is one of those guys who buys top-of-the-line everything but never uses it. He’s got this massive riding lawnmower for a yard that takes ten minutes to push-mow. So I’m on his fancy ladder, right? And this squirrel—I swear to you, the biggest squirrel I have ever seen—jumps out of the oak tree right onto the roof, maybe three feet from my face. It scared the living daylights out of me. I almost dropped the trowel right into the rhododendrons. Those bushes have been a nightmare to keep alive, by the way. The soil in my yard is just terrible, mostly clay. I tried mixing in some of that expensive compost last spring, but it just washed away when the sprinklers... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Something about the flight being smooth? Yeah, I don't know. I just don't like being on them at all.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the plane crashing, are you specifically afraid of dying in a crash, or are there other outcomes that scare you just as much, like being injured or stranded somewhere?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the dying part. I came in here because just the thought of getting on an airplane now scares me so bad that I know it doesn't make sense, but my body just tells me that plane is going to fall right out of the sky. \n\nWhich is wild, because I know the statistics. I'm a doctor, I look at data all day. But speaking of falling, did I tell you about the time I tried to fix the ceiling fan in my living room? It was making this clicking noise, just click, click, click, every time it spun around. Drove me absolutely crazy when I was trying to watch TV. I was watching this documentary series about the Roman Empire, which is fascinating, by the way. Did you know they had underfloor heating in their bathhouses? They called it a hypocaust. They'd have these slaves tending fires all day just to keep the tiles warm. Imagine that kind of engineering back then. Anyway, I got up on a chair—just a regular dining room chair, not even a step stool—to see if the fan blades were loose. I reached up, and one of the legs on the chair just snapped. Completely gave out. I went down hard, right onto the coffee table. Bruised my hip so bad I was limping around the clinic for a week. My nurses thought it was hilarious. One of them, Brenda, she actually bought me a plastic toy step stool from a toddler's playset and left it in my office as a joke. Brenda's a character, she's retiring next year and moving to Florida to live near her daughter...\n\nWait, sorry. What were we talking about? The plane crashing, right. Yeah, my body just completely convinces me the plane is going to fall and I'm going to die. It's just overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Besides flying, are there other situations or places where you feel anxious because you might not be able to escape or get help if you started to feel panicky or very uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's just the flying. I mean, I don't get that panicky feeling anywhere else. I can be in a crowded room, an elevator, whatever. It's just planes. \n\nSpeaking of elevators, though, the one at the clinic where I work has been acting up for weeks. It's one of those old hydraulic ones, and every time it stops on the second floor, it does this little bounce. Just a tiny jolt, but it's enough to make your stomach drop. The maintenance guy, Frank, says it's a valve issue, but getting the part is taking forever. Frank is a good guy, but he's always got some excuse. Last week he was telling me about his son's baseball team. They made it to the state championships, which is a big deal, I guess. I never really played baseball. I was more into track in high school. Ran the 400-meter dash. My coach, Mr. Henderson, used to make us run bleachers until we threw up. He was this intense, red-faced guy who always wore those short coaching shorts, even in November. We used to joke that his legs were immune to the cold. I actually ran into him at the grocery store a few years ago. He looked exactly the same, just a little grayer... \n\nSorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? What did you ask? Oh, right, other places making me anxious. No, it's just the flying. Just the airplanes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced sudden episodes of intense fear or panic in situations other than being on or thinking about an airplane?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I don't really get those panic things anywhere else. It's just the planes. \n\nAlthough, speaking of panic, you should have seen my wife last night. We were making dinner, and she was trying a new recipe for this chicken piccata thing. She saw it on one of those cooking shows, the ones where they have twenty minutes to make a gourmet meal out of, like, a shoe and some asparagus. Anyway, she was chopping lemons, and the knife slipped. She didn't cut herself bad, just a tiny nick on her finger, but she absolutely hates the sight of blood. Just hates it. She turned pale as a ghost and had to sit down on the kitchen floor. I had to get her a glass of water and put a band-aid on it. It's funny, because when we first met, she wanted to be a nurse. She actually took a semester of anatomy in college before she realized she couldn't handle the dissection labs. They were working on fetal pigs, I think. Or maybe it was cats. I remember when I was in med school, our gross anatomy lab was in the basement of this old brick building...\n\nWait, sorry. Panic attacks, right? Yeah, no, I don't get them outside of the flying stuff. Just the airplanes.\n\nDoctor: When you imagine being on a plane, is your fear primarily about falling from the sky, or are there other specific aspects of flying that contribute equally to your fear?\n\nPatient: Well, it's really just the falling. I mean, the whole thing makes me anxious now, but the core of it is definitely the falling from the sky part. Just thinking about the ground rushing up... \n\nBut, you know, speaking of falling from the sky, did you see that meteor shower last week? The Perseids, I think they're called. I tried to stay up to watch them. I went out on the back deck around midnight with a blanket and a cup of coffee. The coffee was a mistake, honestly. It was that dark roast my brother-in-law brought back from his trip to Costa Rica. He goes down there every year for a surfing retreat. He's fifty-two and still surfing, which is impressive, I guess, but he's already had two shoulder surgeries. His orthopedic surgeon told him he needs to stop, but he won't listen. Anyway, the coffee kept me up until 3 AM, and I only saw maybe two shooting stars. The light pollution in our neighborhood is just too bad. They installed these new LED streetlights last year, and they are incredibly bright. Like, aggressively bright. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, actually started a petition to have them dimmed. She's this retired librarian who has a petition for everything. Last month it was about the speed limit on our street, and before that, it was about the type of mulch the landscaping company uses in the common areas...\n\nI'm doing it again. I'm sorry. The plane. Right. No, it's not the enclosed space or the turbulence or anything like that. It's just the falling. The crashing. That's the part that terrifies me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, unexpected panic attack when you weren't thinking about flying or being on a plane?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I don't just sit around and suddenly get panicky out of nowhere. It's always tied to the flying thing. \n\nSpeaking of out of nowhere, though, the other day I was driving to work, just minding my own business on the interstate, and this guy in a silver Honda swerves right into my lane without a turn signal. Just completely cut me off. I had to slam on the brakes, and my briefcase, which was on the passenger seat, went flying onto the floorboard. Papers went everywhere. And of course, it was the day I had all the printouts for the new clinic protocols. Our clinic administrator, Brenda, has been on this huge kick about updating our charting system. She went to some seminar in Chicago last month and came back completely obsessed with efficiency metrics. She's been scheduling these mandatory staff meetings every Tuesday at 7 AM. Who wants to talk about efficiency at 7 AM? I'm barely awake. I usually stop at this little bakery on the corner for a bagel and coffee, but even they aren't open that early. The guy who runs it, Sal, he makes these incredible everything bagels from scratch. He told me his grandfather started the recipe back in Brooklyn in the 1950s. They used to have this tiny storefront, and...\n\nWait, sorry. I lost track again. What were we talking about? Panic attacks out of nowhere? Yeah, no, I don't get those. It's really just the airplanes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah, absolutely. It's immediate. Even if I just get an email from the hospital about a rural shift that needs a flight, my heart starts pounding right away. My hands get all sweaty, and I feel like I can't breathe until I close the message. 

It's actually a real problem because we get a lot of those emails lately. The rural outreach program has been expanding. They're trying to cover more clinics in the northern part of the state, which is great for the patients up there, but staffing it is a nightmare. Our scheduling coordinator, Mark, sends out these blast emails every Friday afternoon trying to get coverage. Mark is a good guy, but he has the worst timing. Friday afternoon is when everyone is just trying to wrap up charting and get out the door. I usually spend Friday evenings at my son's baseball games. He's in Little League now, playing shortstop. He's got a pretty good arm, but he struggles with hitting curveballs. His coach, Dave, keeps telling him to wait on the pitch, but he gets too eager. Dave actually played minor league ball for a few years back in the nineties. He was in the Astros organization, I think. Or maybe it was the Rangers. He's got this whole collection of vintage baseball cards he keeps in his garage, which is completely climate-controlled. I went over there once for a barbecue, and he had this massive smoker going...

Sorry, I'm doing it again. The anxiety before a flight. Yeah, it hits me the second I even think about having to get on a plane or book a ticket. Just instantly.

No, honestly, I don't really have any other fears like that. Nothing else makes me feel that way. I mean, I'm pretty calm in general.

He's got a pretty good arm, but he struggles with hitting curveballs. His coach, Dave, keeps telling him to wait on the pitch, but he gets too eager. Dave actually played minor league ball for a few years back in the nineties. He was in the Astros organization, I think. Or maybe it was the Rangers. He's got this whole collection of vintage baseball cards he keeps in his garage, which is completely climate-controlled. I went over there once for a barbecue, and he had this massive smoker going...\n\nSorry, I'm doing it again. The anxiety before a flight. Yeah, it hits me the second I even think about having to get on a plane or book a ticket. Just instantly.\n\nDoctor: When you start feeling anxious about flying, do you notice any physical symptoms like your heart racing, sweating a lot, or having trouble breathing?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... no, not really. I don't think I get those specific things you mentioned, like the sweating or the breathing trouble. I just feel this overwhelming dread, you know? Like this heavy feeling in my stomach. \n\nSpeaking of stomachs, though, I've been having the worst heartburn lately. I think it's because I've been eating lunch at this new Mexican place near the clinic. They have these incredible al pastor tacos, but the salsa verde is just lethal. I went there on Tuesday with Dr. Evans from pediatrics. He's retiring next month, actually, after like thirty-five years. We're throwing him a big party at the country club, which is going to be a whole ordeal because the catering manager there is incredibly difficult to work with. She insisted we finalize the menu three months in advance, and she absolutely refused to let us bring in our own cake. My wife was going to bake her famous lemon raspberry cake—she took this masterclass online last year with this French pastry chef, and she's gotten really into baking. She bought all these specialized pans and a kitchen scale that measures down to the gram. Our kitchen counters are just covered in baking supplies now. I had to move the toaster to the dining room table just to have room to make breakfast...\n\nWait, sorry. Physical symptoms. Right. No, my heart doesn't really race, and I don't get sweaty or anything like that. Just the dread.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other everyday situations or places you actively avoid—not because of fear of flying—but because you worry you might feel trapped, have a panic attack, or need to get help quickly?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't avoid anything else. I mean, I go to the grocery store, I drive, I go to work... it's just the flying that bothers me. I don't worry about being trapped anywhere else. \n\nAlthough, speaking of being trapped, I did get stuck in the elevator at the hospital last week. It was the service elevator in the west wing, the one they're supposed to be replacing. I was in there with Gary from maintenance. Gary is this huge guy, ex-Navy, and he was carrying this massive toolbox. We were stuck between the third and fourth floors for like forty-five minutes. Gary just sat on his toolbox and started telling me about his model train collection. He's got this entire setup in his basement, like, a whole miniature town with working streetlights and everything. He said he spends every weekend painting these tiny little figurines to put on the train platforms. His wife apparently hates it because it takes up the whole basement, and she wanted to turn it into a home gym. She bought this really expensive stationary bike, one of those ones with the screen where the instructors yell at you? My sister has one of those. She uses it like twice a day, but she's always been super competitive. We used to play Monopoly when we were kids, and if she started losing, she would just flip the board over...\n\nOh, wait. Sorry. What was the question? Other situations where I feel trapped? No, no, I'm fine with elevators and crowds and all that. Just airplanes.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations, aside from flying, where you feel a strong sense of fear or anxiety, even if it doesn’t lead to panic or the need to escape?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, I don't really have any other fears like that. Nothing else makes me feel that way. I mean, I'm pretty calm in general. \n\nWell, except for maybe when I'm driving on the interstate and people don't use their turn signals. That drives me absolutely crazy. Like, it's a tiny little lever right next to the steering wheel, how hard is it to just flick it up or down? I was driving behind this guy in a blue Honda CR-V yesterday, and he just drifted across three lanes of traffic without signaling once. He had this bumper sticker that said \"My other ride is a TARDIS,\" which, I mean, I used to watch Doctor Who back when Tom Baker was the Doctor. My dad and I would watch it on PBS on Saturday nights. My dad was an accountant, very numbers-oriented, very serious guy, but for some reason, he loved British sci-fi. He even tried to build a Dalek in our garage once out of cardboard and old trash cans. It looked terrible, honestly, but he was so proud of it. He painted it silver and used a plunger for the arm...\n\nBut yeah, anyway. Other fears or anxiety? No, nothing like the airplane thing. Just normal annoyances, really.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations, aside from flying, where you feel a strong sense of fear or anxiety, even if it doesn't lead to panic or the need to escape?\n\nPatient: I thought we just... didn't you just ask me that? Or maybe I'm getting confused. But no, I really don't have any other big fears or anything like that. It's just the flying. \n\nI guess my memory is a little scattered today. I was up late last night trying to fix the sprinkler system in the front yard. The timer box is completely shot. It's one of those old analog ones from the nineties, with the little plastic pins you have to push in? Well, half the pins are missing, and the dial gets stuck at 4:00 AM, so the sprinklers just run for hours. My water bill last month was astronomical. I called this landscaping company to come look at it, and the guy they sent out—his name was Trevor, I think—he showed up two hours late and tracked mud all over the driveway. He said I need a whole new digital system, which is fine, but then he started trying to upsell me on this smart-home integration thing where you can control the sprinklers from your phone. I told him I barely know how to use my phone for emails, let alone watering the lawn. My kids are always making fun of me for still having an iPhone 8. My daughter actually bought me a new one for my birthday, but it doesn't have a home button, and I just can't get used to swiping up for everything...\n\nWait, sorry. No, I don't have any other fears. Just the airplanes. Everything else is fine."
